<!-- assumption-1: body_md -->
\label{assump:parameters} We assume that $\alpha > \frac{5}{3}$, $\beta \in (\frac{1}{2}, 1)$, $\gamma_0 \in (\frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{2}]$, and $\delta \geq 0$ where the parameters are moreover related by the identity \eqref{eq:gamma-0}.
<!-- end assumption-1 -->
<!-- assumption-2: body_md -->
\label{assump:initial}
We assume that the initial condition $u_0$ of equation \eqref{eq:FSBL} is of one of the following types:
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
    {\begin{enumerate}}{\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  [(i)]
    \item $u_0 - \psi_0 \in \mathcal{C}^1(\mathbb{T})$, where $\psi$ is the stationary solution of the linear equation \eqref{eq:linear}, which is defined in \eqref{eq:stationary_sol}, \label{assump: initial 1st kind}
    \item $u_0 \in \mathcal{C}^{\zeta_0} (\mathbb{T})$ for some $\zeta_0 > \frac{1}{2}$. \label{assump: initial 2nd kind}
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@enumerate} 
    {\end{enumerate}}{\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@enumerate\endcsname[1]\relax}
<!-- end assumption-2 -->
<!-- thm-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:main}
Let Assumptions~\ref{assump:parameters} and \ref{assump:initial} be satisfied. Then for almost every realization of the noise $\xi$ there exists $\bar T \in (0, \infty]$ such that the stochastic fractional Burgers-type equation \eqref{eq:FSBL} has a unique solution $u(t, \cdot) \in \mathcal{C}^\gamma(\mathbb{T})$ on the time interval $t \in [0, \bar T)$, for any $\gamma < \gamma_0$. This solution is defined in the sense of Definition~\ref{def: sol of FSBL}. 

In the case $\bar T < \infty$ this solution satisfies  
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:convergence-lifetime}
\lim_{t \nearrow \bar T} \| u_t - \psi_t \|_{\mathcal{C}^{\widetilde \gamma}} = \infty
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
almost surely for any $\widetilde \gamma > 1$, where $\psi$ is the stationary solution of equation \eqref{eq:linear}. 

If $\|F\|_{\mathcal{C}^1}$ and $\|G\|_{\mathcal{C}^3}$ are bounded, then $\bar T = \infty$ almost surely.
<!-- end thm-1 -->
<!-- defn-1: body_md -->
For $\beta \in (0,1)$ and a fixed point $a \in \mathbb{R}$, \emph{the left-side Riemann--Liouville integral} of order $\beta$ starting at $a$ is defined as 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:RL-integral}
    \bigl(I^{\beta}_{a+} f\bigr)(t) \coloneqq \frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta)} \int_{a}^t \frac{f(r)}{(t-r)^{1-\beta}} \, \mathrm{d} r,
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    for $t > a$ and a continuous function $f : [a, t] \to \mathbb{R}$. Here, $\Gamma(\beta)$ is the Gamma function. In the case $a = 0$ we simply write $I^{\beta} = I^{\beta}_{0+}$.
    
    For an end point $b \in \mathbb{R}$, \emph{the right-side Riemann--Liouville integral} of order $\beta$ ending at $b$ is defined as 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:RL-integral-minus}
    \bigl(I^{\beta}_{b-} f\bigr)(t) \coloneqq \frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta)} \int_t^b \frac{f(r)}{(r-t)^{1-\beta}} \, \mathrm{d} r,
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    for $t < b$ and a continuous function $f : [t, b] \to \mathbb{R}$.
<!-- end defn-1 -->
<!-- defn-2: body_md -->
\label{def: Caputo derivative}
 For $\beta \in (0,1)$ and a starting point $a \in \mathbb{R}$, \emph{the left-side Caputo fractional derivative} of order $\beta$ starting at $a$ is defined as 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:frac-deriv}
    {}^{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf{C}} D^{\beta}_{a+} f (t) \coloneqq \bigl( I^{1-\beta}_{a+} \partial \bigr) f(t) = \frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\beta)} \int_a^t \frac{f'(r)}{(t-r)^\beta} \, \mathrm{d} r,
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    for $t > a$ and a $\mathcal{C}^1$ function $f : [a, t] \to \mathbb{R}$. We denote $\partial^{\beta} f(t) = {}^{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf{C}} D^{\beta}_{0+} f(t)$.
    
    \emph{The left-side Riemann--Liouville fractional derivative} of order $\beta$ starting at $a \in \mathbb{R}$ is defined as 
    $$
    {}^{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf{RL}} D^{\beta}_{a+} f(t) \coloneqq \big( \partial I^{1-\beta}_{a+} \big) f(t) = \frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\beta)} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} \int_a^t \frac{f(r)}{(t-r)^\beta} \, \mathrm{d} r,
    $$
    for $a < t$ and a function $f : [a, t] \to \mathbb{R}$ for which the expression is defined. 
    
    \emph{The right-side Riemann--Liouville fractional derivative} of order $\beta$ ending at $b \in \mathbb{R}$ is defined as 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:RL-derivative}
    {}^{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf{RL}} D^{\beta}_{b-} f(t) \coloneqq - \big( \partial I^{1-\beta}_{b-} \big) f(t) = - \frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\beta)} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} \int_t^b \frac{f(r)}{(r-t)^\beta} \, \mathrm{d} r,
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    for $t < b$ and a function $f : [t,b] \to \mathbb{R}$ for which the expression is defined.
<!-- end defn-2 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{Gubinelli deriv of G(v+x)}
    Let $\mathbf{X} = (X, \mathbb{X}) \in \mathcal{D}^\gamma( [a,b], \mathbb{R}^n)$ be a $\gamma$-rough path, $v \in \mathcal{C}^{2 \gamma}( [a,b], \mathbb{R}^n)$ and $g \in \mathcal{C}^2(\mathbb{R}^n, \mathbb{R}^n)$. Then $(g(X+v), \nabla g(X+v) ) \in \mathcal{C}^\gamma_{\mathbf{X}}$. Moreover, we have the estimates 
         
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align}

  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
          \| R^g \|_{2 \gamma} 
          &\leq \frac{1}{2} \|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^2} \left( \|X\|_{\gamma}^2 + 2 \|v\|_{ {2 \gamma} } \right), \label{bdd: remainder R of G} \\
        \| ( g(X+v), \nabla g(X+v) ) \|_{\mathbf{X}, \gamma} 
        &\leq \frac{1}{2}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^2} \left(4 \|X\|_{\gamma} + \|X\|_{\gamma}^2 + 4 ( \|v\|_{\gamma} + \|v\|_{ {2 \gamma} } )\right), \notag 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align}

  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    where $R^g$ is the remainder \eqref{eq:remainder-rough-path} for this controlled rough path.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Since $g \in \mathcal{C}^{2}$ and $v \in \mathcal{C}^{2 \gamma}$, the function $g(v+X)$ is in $\mathcal{C}^\gamma$ on $[a,b]$. Furthermore, for any $a \leq x < x' \leq b$ we have

        &\big| g(X_{x'} + v_{x'}) - g(X_{x} + v_{x}) - \nabla g(X_{x} + v_{x}) ( X_{x'} - X_{x} ) \big| \\
          &\hspace{3cm} \leq \big| g(X_{x'} + v_{x}) - g(X_{x} + v_{x}) - \nabla g(X_{x} + v_{x}) ( X_{x'} - X_{x} ) \big| \\
        &\hspace{7cm} + \big| g(X_{x'} + v_{x'}) - g(X_{x'} + v_{x}) \big| \\
          &\hspace{3cm} \leq \frac{1}{2} \|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^2} \|X\|_{\gamma}^2 |x' - x|^{2 \gamma} + \|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^1} \|v\|_{ {2 \gamma} } |x' - x|^{2 \gamma}

        \|R^g\|_{2 \gamma}

          &\hspace{1cm} \leq \|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^1} ( \|v\|_\gamma + \|X\|_\gamma ) + \|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^2} ( \|v\|_\gamma + \|X\|_\gamma ) + \frac{1}{2} \|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^2} \|X\|_{\gamma}^2 + 2 \|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^1} \|v\|_{ {2 \gamma} },

    Let $\mathbf{X} = (X, \mathbb{X}) \in \mathcal{D}^\gamma( [a,b], \mathbb{R}^n )$ for some $T > 0$ and $\gamma \in (\frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{2}]$, and let $G: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be a $\mathcal{C}^2$ function with bounded derivatives. Then the rough path integral defined in \eqref{def: rough path integral} exists and has the following bound

          &\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad \leq C \|G\|_{\mathcal{C}^2} \left( \|X\|^3_\gamma + \|X\|_{\gamma} \|\mathbb{X}\|_{2 \gamma} \right) |x'-x|^{3 \gamma},

    &\int_{-\infty}^{t} \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}} \left( - |k|^{\delta} \right) e^{-ikx} \widehat{P}_{\beta, \alpha} (t-s, -k) \overline{\left( - |k|^{\delta} \right) e^{-ik x'} \widehat{P}_{\beta, \alpha} (t'-s, -k)} \,\mathrm{d} s \\
      &\qquad = \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{-\infty}^{t} \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}} e^{-ik(x-x')} |k|^{2 \delta} E_{\beta}(- (t-s)^\beta |k|^\alpha) E_{\beta}(- (t'-s)^\beta |k|^\alpha) \,\mathrm{d} s,

where in the last identity we used formula \eqref{eq:heat-kernel}. The imaginary part of this sum vanishes by antisymmetry. Changing the integration variable, we get \eqref{eq:psi-covariance}.

    Because the expectation of the process vanishes and the covariance function \eqref{eq:psi-covariance} depends on the difference of the time points, the process $\psi$ is stationary in time.

<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
We are going to use Kolmogorov's continuity theorem \cite{MR4226142}, which requires bounding moments of the process. We note that $\psi$ is Gaussian, and it is sufficient to bound only the second moments. More precisely, we will prove that there exists a constant $L = L(\alpha, \beta, \delta)$ such that

  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:psi-bound-1}
\mathbb{E} \big[ | \psi(t, x) - \psi(t, x') |^2 \big] \leq L |x-x'|^{2\gamma'}
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {

  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
and

  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:psi-bound-2}
\mathbb{E} \big[ | \psi(t, x) - \psi(t', x) |^2 \big] \leq L |t-t'|^{2 \beta'},

for any $0 \leq \gamma' < \frac{1}{2}(\frac{\alpha}{\beta} - 2 \delta - 1)$ and $0 \leq \beta' < \frac{\beta}{2 \alpha}(\frac{\alpha}{\beta} - 2 \delta - 1)$ such that $\beta' \leq \frac{\beta}{2}$, and for all spatial and temporal points $x, x' \in \mathbb{T}$ and $t, t' \in [0, T]$. The proportionality constants are independent of the spatial and temporal points. Then Kolmogorov's continuity theorem and interpolation yield \eqref{eq:psi-regularity}. We note that the condition $\beta' \leq \frac{\beta}{2}$ is redundant due to the assumptions on the parameters.

To prove \eqref{eq:psi-bound-1} we use \eqref{eq:psi-covariance} and write

        \mathbb{E} \big[ | \psi(t, x) - \psi(t, x') |^2 \big] = \mathfrak{C}_\beta(0) \frac{2 n}{\pi} \sum_{k=1}^\infty k^{2 \delta - \frac{\alpha}{\beta}} \bigl(1- \cos(k(x-x'))\bigr),

     We use the standard bound $1 - \cos(z) \lesssim |z|^{2 \gamma}$ for any $\gamma \in [0, 1]$ to estimate the preceding sum by a constant times 
    \[
    |x-x'|^{2 \gamma} \sum_{k=1}^\infty k^{2 \delta - \frac{\alpha}{\beta} + 2 \gamma},
    \]
    which is convergent if $2 \gamma < \frac{\alpha}{\beta} - 2 \delta - 1$. By our assumptions, we have $\frac{\alpha}{\beta} - 2 \delta > 1$ which yields the desired bound \eqref{eq:psi-bound-1}.

    Similarly, we can prove \eqref{eq:psi-bound-2}. We take $t < t'$ and use \eqref{eq:psi-covariance} to write

<!-- rem-1: body_md -->
The result of Theorem \ref{lem:reg-of-stationary-sol} demonstrates an interesting phenomenon. In the equation \eqref{eq:FSH}, we take $\delta$ times derivative in space on the forcing term (the space-time white noise), so it is no surprise that the regularity of $\psi$ will decrease by $\delta$ in space. However, the interesting part lies in the regularity in time. In the equation \eqref{eq:FSH}, we take $\beta$ times derivative in time and $\alpha$ times derivative in space. Therefore, heuristically, taking $\beta$ times derivative in time ``equals" taking $\alpha$ times derivative in space. And we take $\delta$ times derivative in space on the forcing term, which ``equals to" taking $\frac{\beta}{\alpha} \delta$ times derivative in time. And this is exactly the amount of regularity $\psi$ gets decreased in time.
<!-- end rem-1 -->

        \mathbb{E} \big[ \vert\!\vert\!\vert \Psi \vert\!\vert\!\vert_{\gamma, T} \big] \leq C.

    Finally, by Kolmogorov's continuity theorem \cite[Theorem~4.23]{MR4226142}, one obtains the estimate $\mathbb{E} \big( \| \uppsi \|_{2 \gamma, T} \big) \lesssim L$. Combining it with Theorem \ref{lem:reg-of-stationary-sol}, we get \eqref{eq:lift-bound}, as desired. 
    
    Now it just remains to show that $\sigma^2$ is concave and non-decreasing on some interval $[0, h]$. We get from \eqref{eq:psi-covariance} the formula

        \sigma^2 ( u ) = \mathfrak{C}_\beta(0) \frac{2 n}{\pi} \sum_{k=1}^\infty k^{2 \delta - \frac{\alpha}{\beta}} \bigl(1- \cos(k u)\bigr),
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

    and the concavity and non-decreasing on $[0, \frac{\pi}{2}]$ follows from a more general result that for any $\rho \in (0,1]$ the function
    \[
    \eta(u) \coloneqq \displaystyle \sum_{k=1}^\infty k^{ - \rho - 1} \big( 1 - \cos ( ku ) \big)

    is concave and non-decreasing on $[0, \frac{\pi}{2}]$. By the Dirichlet test, the derivative of $\eta(u)$ is just the sum of the derivatives of each term

        \eta'(u) = \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{\sin(ku) }{k^\rho},

    Using $\sin(ku) = \frac{e^{iku} - e^{-iku}}{2i}$, we can then rewrite $\eta'$ as

    Recall that the polylogarithm is defined by $\mathrm{Li}_\rho (x) = \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{x^k}{k^\rho}$ for $\rho \in (0, 1]$ and $x \in \{ z \in \mathbb{C} : \|z\| \leq 1 , z \neq 1 \}$, and the following integral representation holds for $\rho > 0, x \in \mathbb{C} \setminus \{ z \in \mathbb{R} : z \geq 1 \}$:

        \mathrm{Li}_\rho (e^{iu}) = \frac{1}{\Gamma(\rho)} \int_0^\infty \frac{x^{\rho-1}}{e^{x-iu} - 1 } \,\mathrm{d} x.

    Then we get

        \eta'(u) &= \frac{1}{2i \Gamma(\rho)} \int_0^\infty x^{\rho-1} \left( \frac{1}{e^{x-iu} -1 } - \frac{1}{e^{x+iu} -1 } \right)\mathrm{d} x \\
        &= \frac{1}{\Gamma(\rho)} \int_0^\infty \frac{1}{x^{1-\rho} } \frac{e^x \sin(u)}{e^{2x} - 2\cos(u) e^x + 1 } \,\mathrm{d} x.

Differentiating, yields

        \eta''(u)
        &= \frac{1}{\Gamma(\rho)} \int_0^\infty \frac{e^x}{x^{1-\rho} } \frac{\cos(u) ( e^{2x} +1 ) - 2 e^x}{( e^{2x} - 2\cos(u) e^x + 1)^2 } \,\mathrm{d} x \\
        &= \frac{1}{\Gamma(\rho)} \int_0^\infty \frac{1}{x^{1-\rho} } \frac{\cos(u) ( e^{x} + e^{-x} ) - 2}{( e^{x} + e^{-x} - 2\cos(u))^2 } \,\mathrm{d} x \\
        &= \frac{\cos(u) }{2 \Gamma(\rho)} \int_0^\infty \frac{1}{x^{1-\rho} } \frac{\cosh(x) - \frac{1}{\cos(u)}}{\big( \cosh(x) - \cos(u) \big)^2 } \,\mathrm{d} x

    for $u \in (0, \frac{\pi}{2})$. Then we get $\eta'' (u) \leq 0$ for $u \in (0, \frac{\pi}{2})$ from the following result: 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {

  \label{eq:integral-is-negative}
        \int_0^\infty \frac{1}{x^{1-\rho}} \frac{\cosh(x) - 1 / v}{( \cosh(x) - v)^2 } \, \mathrm{d} x < 0 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

    for any $\rho, v \in (0,1)$.

    It is left to prove \eqref{eq:integral-is-negative}. We start by proving the following identity:
\[
\int_0^\infty \frac{\cosh(x) - 1/v}{(\cosh(x) - v)^2 } \,\mathrm{d} x = - \frac{1}{v}.
\]
For this, we define the function $f(v) = \int_0^\infty \frac{1}{\cosh(x) - v} \,\mathrm{d} x$ and write the left-hand side as $f(v) + ( v-\frac{1}{v}) f'(v)$. By changing the integration variable to $e^x$, we can compute 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\begin{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        f(v) &= 2 \int_0^\infty \frac{e^x}{e^{2x} + 1 - 2v e^x } \,\mathrm{d} x = 2 \int_1^\infty \frac{1}{x^2 - 2vx + 1 } \,\mathrm{d} x,
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    which is equal to $\frac{2}{ \sqrt{1-v^2} } \arctan( \frac{1+v}{ \sqrt{1-v^2} } )$. Therefore 
\[
f'(v) = \displaystyle \frac{2}{ \sqrt{1-v^2} } \left( \frac{v}{1-v^2} \arctan \left( \frac{1+v}{ \sqrt{1-v^2} } \right) + \frac{1}{2 \sqrt{1-v^2}} \right)
\]
and we get $f(v) + ( v-\frac{1}{v}) f'(v) = - \frac{1}{v}$, as desired.

Let us now look at the integral in \eqref{eq:integral-is-negative}. Setting $x^* \coloneqq \cosh^{-1} \left( \frac{1}{v} \right)$, we write the integral as
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
   
        &\int_0^{x^*} \frac{1}{x^{1-\rho}} \frac{\cosh(x) - 1/v}{( \cosh(x) - v)^2 } \, \mathrm{d} x + \int_{x^*}^\infty \frac{1}{x^{1-\rho}} \frac{\cosh(x) - 1/v}{( \cosh(x) - v)^2 } \, \mathrm{d} x \\
        &\qquad \leq \int_0^{x^*} \frac{1}{(x^*)^{1-\rho}} \frac{\cosh(x) - 1/v}{( \cosh(x) - v)^2 } \, \mathrm{d} x + \int_{x^*}^\infty \frac{1}{(x^*)^{1-\rho}} \frac{\cosh(x) - 1/v}{(\cosh(x) - v)^2 } \, \mathrm{d} x \\
        &\qquad = \frac{1}{(x^*)^{1-\rho}} \int_0^\infty \frac{\cosh(x) - 1 / v}{( \cosh(x) - v)^2 } \, dx = - \frac{1}{(x^*)^{1-\rho}} \frac{1}{v} < 0,
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    where we used $\cosh(x) - \frac{1}{v} \leq 0$ when $x \in [0,x^*]$.

<!-- assumption-3: body_md -->
\label{assump:function-phi}
The function $\phi: \mathbb{R}_+ \to \mathbb{R}$ is positive, strictly decreasing, and has the following form and decay: 
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {

  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:assumption-on-phi}
        \left| \frac{\mathrm{d}^j}{\mathrm{d} x^j} \phi(x) \right| \leq \frac{C}{ 1+ x^{\ell_\star+j}} 
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
   
for all $x \in \mathbb{R}_+$ and $j \in \mathbb{N}_0$, and for some $\ell_\star \in \mathbb{N}$. Moreover, $-\phi'(x)$ is positive and decreasing on $x \geq 0$.
<!-- end assumption-3 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{L^1-bound of P}
    If $m \in \mathbb{N}_0$ satisfies $m < \alpha \ell_\star - 1$, then there is a constant $C=C(m) > 0$ such that
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        \| \partial_x^m  P_t \|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} \leq C t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} m } 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {\end{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    uniformly in $t > 0$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Using the Fourier series and \eqref{eq:P-Fourier}, we write 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq1}
        \partial_x^m P(t, x) = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} (ik)^m \widehat{P}_t(k) e^{ikx} = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} (ik)^m \phi(t^\beta |k|^\alpha) e^{ikx}.
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    The desired bound will follow from bounds on the function $P$ in the two regimes: $|x|<t^{\beta / \alpha}$ and $|x|\geq t^{\beta / \alpha}$, which require different analysis. 

    In the case $|x|<t^{\beta / \alpha}$, we simply use the assumption \eqref{eq:assumption-on-phi} and get
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\begin{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        | \partial_x^m P(t, x) | 
         \leq \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} |k|^m \phi(t^\beta |k|^\alpha) \lesssim \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} \frac{|k|^m}{1 + (t^\beta |k|^\alpha)^{\ell_\star}}.
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    Estimating the sum by the respective integral, we bound this expression by a constant times 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
    \int_0^\infty \frac{k^m}{1 + (t^\beta k^\alpha)^{\ell_\star}} \, \mathrm{d} k = t^{-\frac{\beta}{\alpha} (m+1) } \int_0^{\infty} \frac{k^m}{1+ k^{\alpha \ell_\star}} \, \mathrm{d} k \lesssim t^{-\frac{\beta}{\alpha} (m+1)}, \label{eq2}
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    where we rescaled the integration variable by $t^{\beta/\alpha}$. The integral converges because of our assumption $\alpha \ell_\star > m + 1$. 

    In the case $|x| \geq t^{\beta / \alpha}$, we define the discrete Laplacian 
    \[
    \underline{\Delta}\, g(k) \coloneqq g(k+1) - 2 g(k) + g(k-1).
    \]
    Then we have $\underline{\Delta}\, e^{ikx} = - 4\sin^2 (x / 2) e^{ikx}$. Expressing $e^{ikx}$ from the right-hand side and substituting into \eqref{eq1}, we get
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:P-discrete-Laplace}
        \partial_x^m P(t, x) = - \frac{1}{4\sin^2 (x / 2)} \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} (ik)^m \phi(t^\beta |k|^\alpha) \left( \underline{\Delta} e^{ikx} \right). 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    We note that the discrete Laplacian satisfies the summation by parts formula 
    \[
    \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} f(k) \underline{\Delta} g(k) = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} \underline{\Delta} f(k) g(k),
    \]
    provided the sums converge. Applying it to the preceding expression, we get 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:P-bound-1}
        | \partial_x^m P(t, x) | \leq \frac{1}{4\sin^2 (x / 2)} \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} \left| \underline{\Delta} \left[ k^m \phi(t^\beta |k|^\alpha) \right] \right|. 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    We bound $\underline{\Delta} \left[ k^m \phi(t^\beta |k|^\alpha) \right]$ using the mean value theorem. For this, we need to distinguish the cases $k=0$ and $k \neq 0$.

    For $k=0$ we have 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:Delta-phi-k0}
        | \underline{\Delta} \left[ k^m \phi(t^\beta |k|^\alpha) \right](0) | = | 1^m \phi(t^\beta) - 2 \cdot 0^m \phi(0) + (-1)^m \phi(t^\beta) |
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    with the convention $0^0=1$. The latter vanishes if $m$ is odd. If $m$ is a non-zero even, then we bound \eqref{eq:Delta-phi-k0} by $2 | \phi(t^\beta) | \lesssim \frac{1}{1+ t^{\beta \ell_\star}} \lesssim t^{- \beta \ell_\star} \wedge 1$, where we made use of our assumption \eqref{eq:assumption-on-phi}. In the case $m=0$ we use the mean value theorem to bound \eqref{eq:Delta-phi-k0} by 
    \[
    2| t^\beta \phi'(t^\beta \eta) | \lesssim \frac{t^\beta}{1+ \eta^{(\ell_\star+1)} t^{\beta(\ell_\star+1)}} \wedge 1 \lesssim t^\beta \wedge 1 \lesssim t^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}
    \]
    for some $\eta \in [0, 1]$, where we used our assumption \eqref{eq:assumption-on-phi} again. In all these cases, we can bound \eqref{eq:Delta-phi-k0} by a constant times $t^{-\frac{\beta}{\alpha} (m-1)}$ since $\alpha > 1$ and $m < \alpha \ell_\star -1$. 

    For $|k| \geq 1$ we consider only $k \geq 1$, while the analysis for $k \leq -1$ is the same. We apply the mean value theorem to bound 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        | \underline{\Delta} \left[ k^m \phi(t^\beta k^\alpha) \right] | \lesssim \left| \frac{\mathrm{d}^2}{\mathrm{d} \eta^2} \left[ \eta^m \phi(t^\beta \eta^\alpha) \right] \right|
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    for some point $\eta \in [k-1, k+1]$. Computing the derivative and using the assumption \eqref{eq:assumption-on-phi}, we bound this expression by a constant multiple of

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        &|\eta|^{m-2} \phi(t^\beta \eta^\alpha) + t^\beta |\eta|^{m + \alpha-2} |\phi'(t^\beta \eta^\alpha)| + t^{2\beta} |\eta|^{m + 2 \alpha-2} |\phi''(t^\beta \eta^\alpha)| \\
        &\qquad \lesssim \frac{m(m-1) k^{m-2}}{1+ (t^\beta k^\alpha)^{\ell_\star}} + \frac{m t^\beta k^{m + \alpha-2}}{1+ (t^\beta k^\alpha)^{\ell_\star+1}} + \frac{t^{2\beta} k^{m + 2 \alpha-2}}{1+ (t^\beta k^\alpha)^{\ell_\star+2}}.

    Using the preceding estimates, we bound \eqref{eq:P-bound-1} by a constant times 
    \[
   \frac{1}{4\sin^2 (x / 2)} \left( t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} (m-1) } + \sum_{k \neq 0} \left( \frac{m(m-1) k^{m-2}}{1+ (t^\beta k^\alpha)^{\ell_\star}} + \frac{m t^\beta k^{m + \alpha-2}}{1+ (t^\beta k^\alpha)^{\ell_\star+1}} + \frac{t^{2\beta} k^{m + 2 \alpha-2}}{1+ (t^\beta k^\alpha)^{\ell_\star+2}}\right) \right).
    \]
    Estimating the sum by the respective integral and rescaling the integration variable by $t^{\beta/\alpha}$, we get a bound of order
    \[
    \frac{t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} (m-1) }}{\sin^2 (x / 2)} \left( 1 + \int_{0}^\infty \left( \frac{m(m-1) k^{m-2}}{1+ k^{\alpha \ell_\star}} + \frac{m k^{m + \alpha-2}}{1+ k^{\alpha(\ell_\star+1)}} + \frac{k^{m + 2 \alpha-2}}{1+ k^{\alpha(\ell_\star+2)}}\right) \mathrm{d} k \right).
    \]
    The expression in the parentheses is finite, and we get

        \| \partial_x^m P(t, \cdot) \|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} 
        &= \int_{|x|< (t^{\beta / \alpha} \wedge \pi)} | \partial_x^m P(t, x) | \, \mathrm{d} x + \int_{t^{\beta / \alpha} < |x| \leq \pi} | \partial_x^m P(t, x) | \, \mathrm{d} x \\
        &\lesssim t^{-\frac{\beta}{\alpha} m} + t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} (m-1) } \int_{t^{\beta / \alpha} < |x| \leq \pi} \frac{\mathrm{d} x}{\sin^2 (x / 2)}.

    We use the inequality $\sin(\theta) \geq \frac{\theta}{2}$ for $0 \leq \theta \leq \frac{\pi}{2}$ to estimate the preceding expression by

    \]
    which is of order $t^{-\frac{\beta}{\alpha} m}$.

<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Using Lemma~\ref{L^1-bound of P}, we have a trivial bound 
\[
\| \partial_x^m  ( P_{t'} - P_t ) \|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} \leq \| \partial_x^m P_{t'} \|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} + \| \partial_x^m P_t \|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} \lesssim (t')^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} m } + t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} m } \lesssim t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} m }.
\]
If $t' \geq 2t$ then $t'-t \geq t$ and we can bound it by $(t'-t)^\varepsilon t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} m - \varepsilon}$ for any $\varepsilon \geq 0$.

Let us now consider the case $t < t' < 2t$. Let $Q_{t,t'}(x) := P(t', x) - P(t, x)$. Similarly to the proof of Lemma~\ref{L^1-bound of P}, we write

  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
                  
        \partial_x^m Q_{t,t'}(x) = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} (ik)^m \widehat{ Q_{t,t'} }(k) e^{ikx} = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} (ik)^m ( \phi((t')^\beta |k|^\alpha) - \phi(t^\beta |k|^\alpha) ) e^{ikx},
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {

    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
    {

    {\end{equation*}}{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    and as in the preceding proof, we bound this function in different ways in different regimes. 

    For $|x|<t^{\beta / \alpha}$, we use the assumption \eqref{eq:assumption-on-phi} and get

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        | \partial_x^m Q_{t,t'}(x) | 
        &\leq \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} |k|^m | \phi((t')^\beta |k|^\alpha) - \phi(t^\beta |k|^\alpha) | \\
          &\lesssim \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} |k|^m \frac{ ((t')^\beta - t^\beta) |k|^\alpha }{ 1 + (t^\beta |k|^\alpha)^{\ell_\star+1} } \lesssim (t'-t)^{\beta} \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} \frac{ |k|^{m + \alpha} }{ 1 + (t^\beta |k|^\alpha)^{\ell_\star+1} } .
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax}

    As in \eqref{eq2}, we bound this expression by a constant multiple of

        \lesssim (t'-t)^{\beta} t^{-\frac{\beta}{\alpha} (m + \alpha +1)}. \label{eq2-new}

Recalling that we consider $t < t' < 2t$, we have $t'-t < t$ and hence

for any $\varepsilon \in [0, \beta]$. The bound \eqref{bdd: L^1-norm of P_t - P_t'} follows from \eqref{eq2-new} and \eqref{eq3-new} in the same way as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{L^1-bound of P}.

<!-- defn-3: body_md -->
\label{def:concave-down}
We call a sequence $\{ a_k \}_{k=L}^M$ ``concave down" if there exists an integer $L \leq k_0 \leq M$ such that 
\[
a_L \leq a_{L+1} \leq \cdots \leq a_{k_0}, \qquad\qquad a_{k_0} \geq a_{k_0+1} \geq \cdots \geq a_{M}.
\]
<!-- end defn-3 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{L^1-bound_for_sum_of_cos/sin}
    For two natural numbers $L \leq M$, 
    suppose that $\{ b_k \}_{k=L}^M$ is an increasing positive sequence and $\{ c_k \}_{k=L}^M$ is a decreasing positive sequence. Let $\{ a_k \}_{k=L}^M$ be a $[0,1]$-valued ``concave down" sequence. Then there is a universal constant $C > 0$, such that

    and

<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Let us first prove \eqref{eq:sum-of-cosines} in the case when the sequence $a_k$ takes only the value $1$. We use summation by parts to separate $b_k$'s out:
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:sum-of-cosines-0}
\sum_{k=L}^M b_k c_k \cos(kx) = b_L \sum_{k=L}^M c_k \cos(kx) + \sum_{l=L+1}^M ( b_l - b_{l-1} ) \sum_{k=l}^M c_k \cos(kx).
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
Since the sequence $b_k$ is increasing, we bound the $L^1$-norm of this function by 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {\begin{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        &b_L \left\| \sum_{k=L}^M c_k \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} + \sum_{l=L+1}^M ( b_l - b_{l-1} ) \left\| \sum_{k=l}^M c_k \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} \notag \\
        &\hspace{7cm} \leq b_M \sup_{L \leq l \leq M} \left\| \sum_{k=l}^M c_k \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})}. \label{eq:sum-of-cosines-1}
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {

    {\end{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    We apply summation by parts again and get 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {\begin{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        \left\| \sum_{k=l}^M c_k \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} 
        &= \left\| \sum_{m=l}^{M-1} ( c_m - c_{m+1} ) \sum_{k=l}^m \cos(k\, \cdot) + c_M \sum_{k=l}^M \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} \notag \\
        &\leq \sum_{m=l}^{M-1} ( c_m - c_{m+1} ) \left\| \sum_{k=l}^m \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} + c_M \left\| \sum_{k=l}^M \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} \notag \\
        &\leq c_l \sup_{l \leq m \leq M} \left\| \sum_{k=l}^m \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})}, \label{eq:sum-of-cosines-2}

    where the last bound holds because the sequence $c_k$ is decreasing. To estimate the $L^1$ norm, we need two different bounds for the sum. The first one is a trivial bound

        \left| \sum_{k=l}^m \cos(kx) \right| \leq \sum_{k=l}^m \left| \cos(kx) \right| \leq m-l+1. 
        \label{sum_cos_bound_for_x_small}
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

    The second one uses a trigonometric identity 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

        \sum_{k=l}^m \cos(kx) = \frac{ \sin ( ( m+ \frac{1}{2} ) x ) - \sin ( ( l- \frac{1}{2} ) x ) }{2 \sin(x / 2)}

    for $x \notin 2 \pi \mathbb{Z}$. 
    Combining it with the inequality $| \sin (x/2)| \geq \frac{|x|}{4}$ for $x \in [-\pi, \pi]$, we have a second bound

    for $x \neq 0$. 

    Using the two bounds \eqref{sum_cos_bound_for_x_large} and \eqref{sum_cos_bound_for_x_small} on $x$ large and small, respectively, we have the following estimate: 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

        \left\| \sum_{k=l}^m \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} 
        &= \int_{ |x| \leq \frac{1}{m-l+1}} \left| \sum_{k=l}^m \cos(kx) \right| \,\mathrm{d} x + \int_{  \frac{1}{m-l+1} \leq |x| \leq \pi } \left| \sum_{k=l}^m \cos(kx) \right| \, \mathrm{d} x \\
        &\leq \int_{ |x| \leq \frac{1}{m-l+1}} (m-l+1) \,\mathrm{d} x + 2 \int_{\frac{1}{m-l+1} }^\pi \frac{4}{x} \, \mathrm{d} x \\
        &= 2 + 8 \log \left( (m-l+1) \pi \right).

    Applying this estimate to \eqref{eq:sum-of-cosines-2} and using \eqref{eq:sum-of-cosines-0}-\eqref{eq:sum-of-cosines-1}, we get 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation}

  \label{eq:sum-of-cosines-proof}
        \left\| \sum_{k=L}^M b_k c_k \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} \leq C b_M c_L \bigl(1 + \log(M-L+1)\bigr).
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {

    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  

    Now, we will prove \eqref{eq:sum-of-cosines} for a ``concave down" sequence $a_k$. Let $L \leq k_0 \leq M$ be a value as in Definition~\ref{def:concave-down}. Then 
    \[
    \left\| \sum_{k=L}^M a_k b_k c_k \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} \leq \left\| \sum_{k=L}^{k_0} a_k b_k c_k \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} + \left\| \sum_{k=k_0+1}^M a_k b_k c_k \cos(k\, \cdot) \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})}.
    \]
    For $L \leq k \leq k_0$, the sequence $a_k$ is increasing, and we can bound the first $L^1$ norm on the right-hand side by \eqref{eq:sum-of-cosines-proof}, but with the sequence $a_k b_k$ in place of $b_k$. Furthermore, for $k_0+1 \leq k \leq M$, the sequence $a_k$ is decreasing, and we bound the last $L^1$ norm by \eqref{eq:sum-of-cosines-proof} but replacing $c_k$ by $a_k c_k$. Hence, we bound the preceding expression by 
    \[
    C a_{k_0} b_{k_0} c_L \bigl(1 + \log(k_0-L+1)\bigr) + C b_M a_{k_0+1} c_{k_0+1} \bigl(1 + \log(M-k_0)\bigr).
    \]
    Recalling that the values $a_k$ are bounded by $1$, and the sequences $b_k$ and $c_k$ are monotonic, we estimate this expression by 
    \[
    C b_{M} c_L \Bigl(2 + \log(k_0-L+1) + \log(M-k_0)\Bigr) \leq 2 C b_{M} c_L \bigl(1 + \log(M-L+1)\bigr),
    \]
    which is the desired bound \eqref{eq:sum-of-cosines}.

    The bound \eqref{eq:sum-of-sines} is proved in exactly the same way, but using the identity

        \sum_{k=l}^m \sin(kx) = \frac{ \cos ( ( l- \frac{1}{2} ) x ) - \cos ( ( m+ \frac{1}{2} ) x ) }{2 \sin (x/2)}

<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{Sch-est w/o t}
    Let $\gamma \in [0, \infty)$ and $f \in \mathcal{C}^\gamma (\mathbb{T})$. Then for any $m \in \mathbb{N}_0$ satisfying $m < \gamma + \alpha \ell_\star$, any $\widetilde{\gamma} \in (0, \infty) \setminus \mathbb{N}$ satisfying $0 \leq \widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma + m < \alpha \ell_\star$ and any $\kappa \in (0, \gamma - \widetilde{\gamma} + \alpha \ell_\star - m]$ one has 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:Sch-est w/o t}
        \left\| \partial_x^m (P_t * f) \right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}} \leq C t^{- \frac{\beta}{\alpha} ( \widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma + m + \kappa ) } \| f \|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}}
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    uniformly over $t > 0$, where $C = C(\kappa, m) > 0$. 

    Furthermore, for any $\varepsilon \in [0, \beta]$ one has 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:Sch-est w/o t for t-t'}
        \left\| \partial_x^m (P_{t'} - P_t) * f \right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}} \leq C (t'-t)^{\varepsilon} t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} ( \widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma +m +\kappa) - \varepsilon} \| f \|_{\mathcal{C}^\gamma},
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {

  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    uniformly over $0 < t < t'$.

<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
We are going to use the characterization of the spaces $\mathcal{C}^\gamma$ in terms of the Littlewood--Paley theory as explained in Appendix~\ref{sec:besov space}. We have 
\[
\left\| \partial_x^m (P_t * f) \right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}} \lesssim \left\| P_t * f \right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\widetilde{\gamma} + m}},
\]
and we need to estimate the Littlewood--Paley blocks $\Delta_j ( P_t * f)$, what we will do in the two cases: $t < 2^{ -j \frac{\alpha}{\beta} }$ and $t \geq 2^{ -j \frac{\alpha}{\beta} }$.

First, we deal with the case $t < 2^{ -j \frac{\alpha}{\beta} }$. We have
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        \| \Delta_j ( P_t * f) \|_{L^\infty}
        = \left\| \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z} } \varphi(2^{-j} k) \widehat{P_t}(k) \widehat{f}(k) e^{ik \cdot } \right\|_{L^\infty} = \left\| P_t \ast \Delta_j f \right\|_{L^\infty} \leq \left\| P_t \right\|_{L^1} \left\| \Delta_j f \right\|_{L^\infty} \label{eq: block bound-t small},
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    where the last bound holds by Young's convolution inequality. Recalling that $\left\| \Delta_j f \right\|_{L^\infty} \leq 2^{-\gamma j} \| f \|_{\mathcal{C}^\gamma}$, we estimate it furthermore by  
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\begin{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        2^{-\gamma j} \left\| P_t \right\|_{L^1} \| f \|_{\mathcal{C}^\gamma} &\lesssim 2^{-\gamma j} \| f \|_{\mathcal{C}^\gamma},
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    where we used Lemma~\ref{L^1-bound of P} with the value $m=0$. Since $2^j < t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} }$, we conclude that
    \[
    2^{ j (\widetilde{\gamma} + m)} \left\| \Delta_j (P_t * f ) \right\|_{L^\infty} \lesssim 2^{(\widetilde{\gamma} + m-\gamma) j} \| f \|_{\mathcal{C}^\gamma} \lesssim t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} ( \widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma + m ) } \| f \|_{\mathcal{C}^\gamma}
    \]
    for $\widetilde{\gamma} + m \geq \gamma$.

     In the case $t \geq 2^{ -j \frac{\alpha}{\beta} }$, we bound the Littlewood--Paley block in the following way: 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {\begin{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        \| \Delta_j (P_t * f) \|_{L^\infty}
        &= \left\| \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z} } \varphi(2^{-j} k) \widehat{P_t}(k) \widehat{f}(k) e^{ikx} \right\|_{L^\infty} \notag \\
          &= \left\| \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z} } \varphi(2^{-j} k) \widetilde{\varphi}(2^{-j} k) \widehat{P_t}(k) \widehat{f}(k) e^{ikx} \right\|_{L^\infty} \notag \\
        &= \| \Delta_j P_t \ast \widetilde{\Delta}_j f \|_{L^\infty} \leq \| \Delta_j P_t \|_{L^1} \| \widetilde{\Delta}_j f \|_{L^\infty}, \label{eq: block bound-t big}
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {\end{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    where we again used Young's convolution inequality. Here, $\widetilde{\Delta}_j f$ denotes the Littlewood--Paley block associated with a different dyadic partition of unity, determined by $\widetilde{\varphi}$ and $\widetilde{\chi}$, chosen such that $\widetilde{\varphi}$ and $\widetilde{\chi}$ are identically equal to $1$ on the supports of $\varphi$ and $\chi$, respectively. One can see the explanation in Remark~\ref{rem: dyadic partition of unity}.  

To proceed with the proof, we need the following estimate
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        \left\| \Delta_j P_t \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} \leq C \frac{1 + \log 2^j}{1+ (t^{\beta} 2^{j \alpha})^{\ell_\star}}
        \label{eq:L^1-norm of LP-block  of derivative of P}
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    for any $j \geq -1$ and with a constant $C > 0$. Let us now prove it.
    
    For $j=-1$, we can bound it directly by a constant. For $j \geq 0$, we use the definition of the Littlewood--Paley block \eqref{eq:PL-block} and \eqref{eq:P-Fourier} to write
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {\begin{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:P-block-proof}
        \Delta_j P_t (x) 
        &= \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} \varphi(2^{-j} k) \widehat{P} ( t , k) e^{ikx} 
        = 2 \displaystyle\sum_{ \frac{3}{4} 2^j \leq k \leq \frac{8}{3} 2^{j} } \varphi(2^{-j} k) \phi( t^\beta k^\alpha) \cos(kx),
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {\end{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
where we used the fact that $\varphi$ is supported in the annulus $\{ \xi \in \mathbb{R} : \frac{3}{4} \leq |\xi| \leq \frac{8}{3} \}$. Moreover, $\varphi$ may be chosen to be increasing on $[0,\xi_0]$ and decreasing on $[\xi_0, \infty)$ for some $\xi_0 \in \mathbb{R}$. For example, the cutoff function constructed in Appendix~\ref{sec:besov space} has these properties. In addition, $\phi$ is decreasing by the assumption made at the beginning of this section. Thus we apply Lemma~\ref{L^1-bound_for_sum_of_cos/sin} with $a_k = \varphi(2^{-j} k)$, $b_k = 1$, and $c_k = \phi( t^\beta k^\alpha)$ to get 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\begin{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        \left\| \Delta_j P_t  \right\|_{L^1(\mathbb{T})} 
        &\lesssim \phi( t^\beta ( \tfrac{3}{4} 2^{j} )^\alpha ) \big( 1 + \log 2^{j} \big).
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
   
    Using \eqref{eq:assumption-on-phi}, we estimate it by a constant times $\frac{1 + \log 2^{j}}{1+ (t^{\beta} 2^{j \alpha})^{\ell_\star}}$ and get the bound \eqref{eq:L^1-norm of LP-block  of derivative of P}.
    
     Now we go back to the proof of the lemma. Applying \eqref{eq:L^1-norm of LP-block  of derivative of P} to \eqref{eq: block bound-t big} yields 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\begin{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        2^{ j (\widetilde{\gamma} + m)} \left\| \Delta_j ( P_t * f) \right\|_{L^\infty} &\lesssim 2^{ j (\widetilde{\gamma} + m - \gamma)} \frac{1 + \log 2^j}{1+ (t^{\beta} 2^{j \alpha})^{\ell_\star}}  \| f \|_{\mathcal{C}^\gamma} \\
        &\lesssim 2^{j(\widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma - \ell_\star \alpha + m)} t^{ -\ell_\star \beta} \big( 1 + \log 2^{j}) \| f \|_{\mathcal{C}^\gamma},
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    where in the last bound we used our assumption $t^{\beta} 2^{j \alpha} \geq 1$. Bounding $1 + \log 2^j \lesssim 2^{j \kappa}$ for $\kappa > 0$, we estimate the preceding expression by a constant times 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

    Combining the preceding bounds on the Littlewood--Paley blocks of $P_{t} * f$, we get claim \eqref{eq:Sch-est w/o t}.

    To prove \eqref{eq:Sch-est w/o t for t-t'}, we set $c_\star = (\frac{7}{4})^{\alpha/\beta} > 1$ and consider the two cases $t' \geq c_\star t$ and $t' > c_\star t$. This choice of the constant will be clear from our analysis below and will allow us to use Lemma~\ref{L^1-bound_for_sum_of_cos/sin}.

and we need to estimate the Littlewood--Paley blocks of the function inside the norm. As above, we are going to bound the blocks in the two regimes: $t < 2^{ -j \frac{\alpha}{\beta} }$ and $t \geq  2^{ -j \frac{\alpha}{\beta} }$. To use Lemma~\ref{L^1-bound_for_sum_of_cos/sin}, as we did above, we need the sequence  $c_k = \phi( t^\beta k^\alpha) - \phi( (t')^\beta k^\alpha)$ to be positive and decreasing. To see this, we write 
\[
c_k = - \int_{t^\beta k^\alpha}^{(t')^\beta k^\alpha} \phi'(\lambda)\, \mathrm{d} \lambda.

By Assumption~\ref{assump:function-phi}, the function $-\phi'(\lambda)$ is positive and hence the sequence $c_k$ is positive. Moreover, our choice of $c_\star$ yields $(t')^\beta k^\alpha \leq t^\beta (k+1)^\alpha$ for any $\frac{3}{4} 2^j \leq k \leq \frac{8}{3} 2^{j}$, and the integration intervals for different $c_k$ do not intersect. Since $-\phi'(\lambda)$ is decreasing, we get $c_k \geq c_{k+1}$. Hence, Lemma~\ref{L^1-bound_for_sum_of_cos/sin} yields

where we used \eqref{eq:assumption-on-phi}. Using $1 + \log 2^{j} \lesssim 2^{j\kappa}$ for $\kappa > 0$, we bound it furthermore by a constant multiple of 
\[
(t'-t)^\beta 2^{-j \alpha \ell_\star + j\kappa} t^{- \beta(\ell_\star+1)}.
\]
Recall that we have $t < t' < c_\star t$, which means that $t'-t \leq  (c_\star -1)t$ and this expression is bounded by a constant times
\[
(t'-t)^\varepsilon 2^{-j \alpha \ell_\star + j\kappa} t^{- \beta\ell_\star - \varepsilon}
\]
for any $\varepsilon \in [0,\beta]$. Hence, we get

        2^{ j (\widetilde{\gamma} + m)} \left\| \Delta_j (P_{t'} - P_t) * f \right\|_{L^\infty} 
        &\lesssim (t'-t)^\varepsilon 2^{j(\widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma - \ell_\star \alpha + m + \kappa)} t^{- \beta\ell_\star - \varepsilon} \| f \|_{\mathcal{C}^\gamma}.

Using assumption $t \geq c 2^{ -j \frac{\alpha}{\beta} }$, we bound it by a constant multiple of 
\[
(t'-t)^\varepsilon t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} ( \widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma + m + \kappa ) - \varepsilon} \| f \|_{\mathcal{C}^\gamma},
\]
for any $\kappa \in (0, \gamma - \widetilde{\gamma} + \alpha \ell_\star - m]$.

The estimates on the Littlewood--Paley blocks give the desired bound.

<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{sch est for fractional heat eq}
    Let the values $\gamma, \gamma_{\mathrm{ini}} \in [0, \infty)$, $\widetilde{\gamma} \in (0, \infty) \setminus \mathbb{N}$ and $m \in \mathbb{N}_0$ satisfy $\gamma -m < \widetilde{\gamma} < \min( \gamma, \gamma_{\mathrm{ini}} ) + \alpha -m$ and let $0 < \kappa < \min( \gamma, \gamma_{\mathrm{ini}} ) + \alpha -m - \widetilde{\gamma}$. For a fixed $T > 0$, let $f$ be a continuous function on $[0,T] \times \mathbb{T}$ with $f(0, \cdot) \in \mathcal{C}^{\gamma_{\mathrm{ini}}}( \mathbb{T} )$ and $f(s, \cdot) \in \mathcal{C}^\gamma( \mathbb{T} )$ for all $s \in (0, T]$, and satisfying 
    \[
    \|f\|_{ \mathcal{C}^{\gamma, \lambda}_T} < \infty
    \]
    for some $\lambda \in [0,1)$, where we use the norm \eqref{def: C gamma t-lambda}. Then there is a constant $C = C(\kappa, m)$ such that 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:sch est for fractional heat eq}
        \| \partial_x^m X(t) \|_{\mathcal{C}^{\widetilde{\gamma}} } \leq C \left(t^{ \beta - \frac{\beta}{\alpha}(\widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma + m + \kappa) - \lambda } \|f\|_{ \mathcal{C}^{\gamma, \lambda}_t} + t^{ \beta - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} ( \widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma_{\mathrm{ini}} + m +\kappa ) } \left\| f(0) \right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma_{\mathrm{ini}}}}\right)
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\end{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    holds uniformly over $t \in (0, T]$ and 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {\begin{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:sch est for fractional heat eq for t - t'}
        & \| \partial_x^m X(t) - \partial_x^m X(t') \|_{\mathcal{C}^{\widetilde{\gamma}} } \\
          &\qquad \leq C (t-t')^{\varepsilon} \left((t')^{ \beta - \frac{\beta}{\alpha}(\widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma + m + \kappa) - \lambda - \varepsilon } \|f\|_{ \mathcal{C}^{\gamma, \lambda}_t } + (t')^{\beta - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} ( \widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma_{\mathrm{ini}} +m +\kappa ) - \varepsilon } \left\| f(0) \right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma_{\mathrm{ini}}}} \right) \notag
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {\end{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    holds uniformly over $t' < t \in (0, T]$ for any $\varepsilon > 0$ small enough.

    Furthermore, we have $\partial_x^m X \in \mathcal{C}([0,T]; \mathcal{C}^{ \widetilde{\gamma}}(\mathbb{T}))$ if 
    \[
    \beta - \frac{\beta}{\alpha}(\widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma + m + \kappa) - \lambda > 0, \qquad \beta - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} ( \widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma_{\mathrm{ini}} +m +\kappa ) > 0,
    \]
    and $\partial_x^m X \in \mathcal{C}((0,T]; \mathcal{C}^{ \widetilde{\gamma}}(\mathbb{T}))$ otherwise.

<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
We need to show that $P_{\beta, \alpha}$ satisfies the assumption of Section~\ref{sec:schauder}. From \eqref{eq:heat-kernel} we have the Fourier transform \eqref{eq:P-Fourier} with the function $\phi(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}} E_{\beta}(- x)$. All the properties from Assumption~\ref{assump:function-phi} follow readily from the properties of the Mittag--Leffler function $E_{\beta}$. In particular \eqref{lem:ML-bound} yields \eqref{eq:assumption-on-phi} with $\ell_\star=1$.

Recalling our definition of the function \eqref{eq:heat-solution}, we need to bound the three terms on the right-hand side. Let us denote them by $X_1(t)$, $X_2(t)$ and $X_3(t)$ respectively, so that $X(t) = X_1(t) + X_2(t) - X_3(t)$.

Using the Fourier series, we write $X_1$ as
\[
X_1(t,x) = \frac{1}{ \sqrt{2 \pi} } \int_0^t \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} \widehat{{}^{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf{RL}} D_{t-, s}^{1-\beta} P_{\beta, \alpha}(t-s)}(k) \, \widehat{f}(s,k) e^{ikx}\, \mathrm{d} s.
\]
Since the Fourier transform commutes with the fractional derivative, we use \eqref{eq:heat-kernel} to write it further as 
\[
\frac{1}{2 \pi} \int_0^t \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} {}^{\scriptscriptstyle \mathsf{RL}} D_{t-, s}^{1-\beta} E_{\beta}(- (t-s)^\beta |k|^\alpha) \, \widehat{f}(s,k) e^{ikx}\, \mathrm{d} s.
\]
Using Lemma~\ref{lem:frac_D_of_ML} we compute the fractional derivative and get
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {\begin{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
&\frac{\beta}{2 \pi} \int_0^t \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} (t-s)^{\beta - 1} E'_{\beta}(-(t-s)^\beta |k|^\alpha) \, \widehat{f}(s,k) e^{ikx}\, \mathrm{d} s \notag \\
&\qquad = \frac{\beta}{2 \pi} \int_0^t (t-s)^{\beta - 1} \left(P_{1}(t-s) \ast f(s) \right)(x)\, \mathrm{d} s \label{eq: expression of X_1},
 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {

    {\end{align}}{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
with a kernel $P_1$ defined by its Fourier transform as 
\[
\widehat{P_1}(s,k) = \frac{\beta}{\sqrt{2\pi}} E'_{\beta}(-s^\beta |k|^\alpha).
\]
We note that Lemma~\ref{lem:ML-bound} implies that this kernel has the properties \eqref{eq:P-Fourier} and \eqref{eq:assumption-on-phi} with $\ell_\star=2$. Applying then Lemma \ref{Sch-est w/o t} with the kernel $P_1$ in place of $P$ (we can interchange $\partial_x^m$ and $\int_0^t$ because $\partial_x^m (P_1 \ast f)(x)$ is continuous) we get
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax}

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        \| \partial_x^m X_1(t) \|_{\mathcal{C}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}} 
        &\lesssim \int_0^t (t-s)^{\beta-1 - \frac{\beta}{\alpha}(\widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma + m + \kappa)} \| f(s, \cdot) \|_{\mathcal{C}^\gamma} \, \mathrm{d} s \\
          &\lesssim \int_0^t (t-s)^{\beta-1 - \frac{\beta}{\alpha}(\widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma + m + \kappa)} s^{- \lambda} \|f\|_{ \mathcal{C}^{\gamma, \lambda}_t} \, \mathrm{d} s = C t^{ \beta - \frac{\beta}{\alpha}(\widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma + m + \kappa) - \lambda } \|f\|_{ \mathcal{C}^{\gamma, \lambda}_t}, 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    where the integral converges if $\widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma < \alpha - m - \kappa$ and $\lambda < 1 $ and the constant $C$ is given in terms of the Beta function by 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

        C = B\left( \beta - \frac{\beta}{\alpha}(\widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma + m + \kappa) , 1-\lambda  \right).

    Now, we are going to bound the function $X_2$. We have

    X_2(t,x) &= \lim_{s \nearrow t} \bigl(I_{t-, s}^{\beta} P_{\beta, \alpha}(t-s) \ast f(s) \bigr)(x) = \lim_{s \nearrow t} \frac{1}{ \sqrt{2 \pi} } \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} \widehat{I_{t-, s}^{\beta} P_{\beta, \alpha}(t-s)}(k) \, \widehat{f}(s,k) e^{ikx} \\
    &= \lim_{s \nearrow t} \frac{1}{2 \pi} \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} I_{t-,s}^{\beta} E_{\beta}( - (t-s)^\beta |k|^\alpha) \, \widehat{f}(s,k) e^{ikx},
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    where we used \eqref{eq:heat-kernel} again. Swapping the limit and the sum (because the summation converges uniformly near $t$) and using Lemma~\ref{lem:LM-integral}, which says that the fractional integral of the Mittag--Leffler function vanishes in the limit, we conclude that $X_2(t,x) \equiv 0$. 

    Finally, we will analyze $X_3$. Proceeding as before, we get

<!-- defn-4: body_md -->
\label{def: sol of FSBL}
    A continuous stochastic process $u$ is a mild solution to \eqref{eq:FSBL} on the time interval $[0, T]$ if for any $t \in (0, T]$ it can be written as $u_t = \psi_t + v_t + B_t$, where the process $v$ satisfies  
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {\begin{equation}}{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{contraction_M}
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\begin{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        v(t, x) &= \partial_x \int_0^t \int_{-\pi}^\pi P_{\beta, \alpha}(t-s, x-y) \; \partial_s^{1-\beta} \mathcal{Z}_v(s,y) \, \mathrm{d} y\, \mathrm{d} s \\
          &\qquad+ \int_0^t \int_{-\pi}^\pi P_{\beta, \alpha}(t-s, x-y) \partial_s^{1-\beta} \big[ G ( \psi + v + B) \partial_y v \big] (s,y) \, \mathrm{d} y\, \mathrm{d} s \\
        &\qquad+ \int_0^t \int_{-\pi}^\pi P_{\beta, \alpha}(t-s, x-y) \partial_s^{1-\beta} F (\psi(s,y) + v(s,y) + B_s) \, \mathrm{d} y\, \mathrm{d} s \\
          &\qquad+ \int_{-\pi}^\pi P_{\beta, \alpha}(t, x-y) ( u_0(y) - \psi_0(y)) \, \mathrm{d} y
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
    {\end{aligned}}{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
    {

  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    almost surely.
<!-- end defn-4 -->
<!-- rem-2: body_md -->
The forcing terms considered in \eqref{contraction_M} are not, in general, $\mathcal{C}^1$ in time, and hence the Caputo derivative cannot be applied to them directly in the classical sense. We therefore define all three integrals above via the integration-by-parts formula \eqref{eq:heat-solution}. See the discussion surrounding \eqref{eq:heat-solution} for further details.
<!-- end rem-2 -->

<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
We will restrict all the time variables to the interval $[0, \tau_K]$ and write $t$ throughout the proof. Let us denote $\widetilde{G}_v(t,x) = G(\psi(t,x) + v(t,x) + B_t)$ and $\widetilde{DG}_v(t,x) = DG(\psi(t,x) + v(t,x) + B_t)$. From Lemma~\ref{Gubinelli deriv of G(v+x)}, we know that the process $( \widetilde{G}_v, \widetilde{DG}_v)(t,x)$ is controlled by the rough path $\Psi_t(x)$ as a process in the spatial variable $x$. By the rough path theory \cite[Theorem~4.10]{MR4174393}, for any $a, b \in [-\pi, \pi]$ we have

For any $t \geq 0$ we have

As in the proof of the preceding lemma, we restrict the time variable $t$ to $[0, \tau_K]$. Moreover, it will be convenient not to write it to make the formulas below look lighter. Using the notation from that proof, the process $( \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{G}, \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{DG})$ is controlled by the rough path $\Psi$. Then \eqref{eq8} yields 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    {

  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        \left| \delta_{v, \bar v} \mathcal{Z}(b) - \delta_{v, \bar v} \mathcal{Z}(a) \right| 
        &\leq | \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{G}(a) | | \psi(b) - \psi(a) | + | \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{DG} (a) | | \uppsi(a,b) | \label{eq10} \\
          &\quad + C \left( \| \psi \|_{\gamma_*} \| R^{\delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{G}} \|_{2 \gamma_*} + \| \uppsi \|_{2 \gamma_*} \| \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{DG} \|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma_*}} \right) | b-a |^{3 \gamma_*}. \notag
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align}

  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    By the mean value theorem and \eqref{eq:bound-on-G}, we get $| \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{G} (a) | \leq M^{(G)}_{2K+L} \| v - \bar v \|_{L^\infty}$ and $| \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{DG} (a) | \leq M^{(G)}_{2K+L} \| v-\overline{v} \|_{L^\infty}$. 
    
    Next, we bound the term $\| \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{DG} \|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma_*}}$. Let us denote for brevity $w := \overline{v} - v$. Using the fundamental theorem of calculus, we can write
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{DG}(b) - \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{DG}(a)  
        &= \int_0^1 D^2 G( \psi + v + \lambda w + B )(b) w (b) \, \mathrm{d} \lambda \\
          &\qquad - \int_0^1 D^2 G( \psi + v + \lambda w + B )(a) w (a) \, \mathrm{d} \lambda, 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    {\end{align*}}{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    where we have the matrix-vector products inside the integrals. Thus, by the triangle inequality, 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
    { 
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  
        &\left| \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{DG}(b) - \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{DG}(a) \right| \\
          &\quad \leq | w (b) - w (a) | \int_0^1 \left| D^2 G( \psi + v + \lambda w +B )(b) \right| \mathrm{d} \lambda \\
        &\qquad \qquad + | w (a) | \int_0^1 \left| D^2 G( \psi + v + \lambda w + B )(b) - D^2 G( \psi + v + \lambda w +B )(a) \right| \mathrm{d} \lambda \\
        &\quad \leq \| w \|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma_*}} |b-a|^{\gamma_*} M^{(G)}_{2K+L} + \| w \|_{L^\infty} |b-a|^{\gamma_*} \, M^{(G)}_{2K+L} \big( K + L + \| w \|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma_*}} \big).

        &\leq \| w \|_{L^\infty} \, M^{(G)}_{2K+L} \big(K + L + \| w \|_{L^\infty} \big).

    Similarly, we can write the remainder $R^{\delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{G}}$ as

        = \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{G}(b) - \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{G}(a) - \delta_{v, \bar v} \widetilde{DG}(a) ( \psi(b) - \psi(a) ) \\
          &\qquad= \int_0^1 DG( \psi + v + \lambda w +B )(b) w(b) \, \mathrm{d} \lambda - \int_0^1 DG( \psi + v + \lambda w +B )(a) w(a) \, \mathrm{d} \lambda \\
          &\qquad\qquad\qquad - \left( \int_0^1 D^2 G( \psi + v + \lambda w )(a) w(a) \, \mathrm{d} \lambda \right) ( \psi(b) - \psi(a)).

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    Then by adding and subtracting $\int_0^1 DG( \psi + v + \lambda w +B )(b) w (a) \, \mathrm{d} \lambda$ and using the triangle inequality together with \eqref{eq:bound-on-G}, we get

        \leq \| w \|_{\mathcal{C}^{2 \gamma_*} } |b-a|^{2 \gamma_*} M^{(G)}_{2K+L} \\
          &\qquad + \| w \|_{L^\infty} \int_0^1 \Big| DG( \psi + v + \lambda w +B )(b) - DG( \psi + v + \lambda w +B )(a) \Big. \\
        &\hspace{7cm} - D^2 G( \psi + v + \lambda w +B )(a) ( \psi(b) - \psi(a) ) \Big| \, \mathrm{d} \lambda \\
          &\leq \| w \|_{\mathcal{C}^{2 \gamma_*} } |b-a|^{2 \gamma_*} M^{(G)}_{2K+L} \\
        &\qquad + \| w \|_{L^\infty} \int_0^1 \Bigl( | D^2 G( \psi(b) + v(a) + \lambda w(a) +B ) | \big( | \overline{v}(b) - \overline{v}(a) | + \lambda | w (b) - w (a) | \big) \\
        &\hspace{8cm}  + \frac{1}{2} M^{(G)}_{2K+L} \big( \psi(b) - \psi(a) \big)^2\Bigr) \, \mathrm{d} \lambda.

As in the previous proofs, we restrict $t \in [0, \tau_K]$ and write $t$ everywhere. Recalling the definitions of time $t_K$ in \eqref{stop time t_K}, bound \eqref{eq:bound-on-G} and norm \eqref{def: CB_T norm}, we get

        \| \mathcal{V}_v(t) \|_{L^\infty} 
        \leq M^{(G)}_{2 K+L} \| \partial_y v_t \|_{L^\infty} 
        \leq M^{(G)}_{2 K+L} \| v_t \|_{\mathcal{C}^1} 
        \leq M^{(G)}_{2 K+L} L. 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

        &\| \delta_{v, \bar v} \mathcal{V}(t) \|_{L^\infty}
        \leq \| G(\psi_t + v_t +B_t) - G(\psi_t + \bar v_t +B_t) \|_{L^\infty} \| \partial_y v_t \|_{L^\infty} \\
        &\hspace{4cm} + \| G(\psi_t + \bar v_t +B_t) \|_{L^\infty} \| \partial_y v_t -\partial_y \bar v_t \|_{L^\infty} \\
        &\qquad \leq M^{(G)}_{2 K+L} \| v_t - \overline{v}_t \|_{L^\infty} \| v_t \|_{\mathcal{C}^1} + M^{(G)}_{2 K+L} \| v_t - \overline{v}_t \|_{\mathcal{C}^1} \leq M^{(G)}_{2 K+L} (1+L) \| v_t - \overline{v}_t \|_{\mathcal{C}^1}.

    Using the estimate $\| \delta_{v, \bar v} \mathcal{W} \|_{L^\infty_{t}} \leq M^{(F)}_{2 K+L} \| v - \overline{v} \|_{\mathcal{C}_{t}} \leq M^{(F)}_{2 K+L} \| v - \overline{v} \|_{\mathcal{B}_{t}}$, we get

\label{norm of CM_4}
Let $\widetilde{\gamma} \in (0, \infty) \setminus \mathbb{N}$ such that $0 < \widetilde{\gamma} - \gamma_* < \alpha$. 
    For any $t > 0$ and $0 < \kappa < \kappa_0$, where $\kappa_0$ is defined in \eqref{def: CB_T norm}, we have

  }{\csname pseudo@align\endcsname[0]\relax} 
  \label{eq:M4-bound-1}
        \| \mathcal{M}_4(v) \|_{\mathcal{B}_t} &\leq C \bigl(1 + t^{ \frac{\beta}{\alpha} ( \kappa_0 - \kappa ) } \bigr) \| u_0 - \psi_0 \|_{\mathcal{C}^1}, \\
        \| \mathcal{M}_4(v) \|_{ \widetilde \mathcal{B}_t } &\leq C \bigl(1 + t^{ \frac{\beta}{\alpha} ( \kappa_0 - \kappa ) }\bigr) \bigl(\| u_0 \|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma_*}} + \| \psi_0 \|_{\mathcal{C}^{\gamma_*}}\bigr). 
        \label{eq:M4-bound-2}

        \| \mathcal{M}_4(v) (t) \|_{ \mathcal{C}^{\widetilde{\gamma}} } &\lesssim t^{ - \frac{\beta}{\alpha} ( \widetilde{\gamma} - 1 + \kappa)} \| u_0 - \psi_0 \|_{\mathcal{C}^1}.

  }{\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
  
    Furthermore, Lemma~\ref{L^1-bound of P} yields 
     
  \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

        \| \mathcal{M}_4(v) (t) \|_{\mathcal{C}^1} \leq \| P_{\beta, \alpha}(t) \|_{L^1} \| u_0 - \psi_0 \|_{\mathcal{C}^1} \lesssim \| u_0 - \psi_0 \|_{\mathcal{C}^1}.

    The bound \eqref{eq:M4-bound-2} is proved in the same way, but using Lemma~\ref{Sch-est w/o t} with $\gamma = \gamma_*$.
